People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. It can make me run a little late, but I still get myself there.\n\nDoctor: When you check the mirror and apply lotion, are you trying to make yourself look perfect or are you trying to stop yourself from looking a certain way that you think is unacceptable?\n\nPatient: It’s more like I’m trying not to look bad, if that makes sense. I don’t think I’m trying to look perfect. Before a class or one of the senior-center workshops, I’ll check the bathroom mirror a few times, put lotion on my hands, turn my face different ways, and sometimes ask a coworker if I look all right. It makes me run a little behind sometimes, but I still go.\n\nDoctor: Is there one particular part of your body that you're most concerned about?\n\nPatient: Mostly my hands. Since I started helping with the safety trainings, I notice how wrinkly and uneven they look when I’m pointing at things. And then sometimes it shifts to my face, like in the newsletter photos I think it looks crooked, even though people tell me they don’t see it.\n\nDoctor: Do these thoughts about your appearance come into your mind against your will?\n\nPatient: Yes, they do. I don’t really mean to think about it, but it just pops in my head. Since I started helping with those safety trainings, I keep noticing my hands when I’m pointing things out, like how wrinkly or uneven they look. And then it’ll move to my face in those newsletter photos, like I think it looks crooked, even when other people say they don’t see it.\n\nDoctor: Do these thoughts about your appearance interfere with your daily life or cause you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: It does bother me, yes. It hasn’t stopped me from going to work or volunteering, but I’m slower getting ready before trainings because I keep checking. And I’ve started avoiding photos when I can, or I hesitate before going to social things I used to just enjoy without thinking so much.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find it difficult to stop thinking about your appearance once these thoughts start?\n\nPatient: Yes. Once I notice it, it’s hard to let it go. Like with my hands during the safety trainings, I’ll keep thinking about how wrinkly or uneven they look when I’m pointing at things. Then it can switch to my face in those newsletter photos, thinking it looks crooked, even though people tell me they don’t see it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

From the dialogue so far and your current candidates, ask the one follow-up question that best separates them.\n\n[Current Turn]\n12\n\n[Current Candidates]\nF45.2, F42.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I came in because I’ve been getting really stuck on how I look. Since I started helping with the safety trainings at work, I keep noticing my hands when I’m pointing things out—how wrinkly and uneven they look. And then I’ll see my face in the company newsletter photos and think it looks crooked. Other people tell me they don’t see it, but I can’t seem to stop thinking about it.\n\nDoctor: Are you worried about anything else besides how you look?\n\nPatient: Not really anything else I can think of. It’s mostly been about the way I look, especially when I notice it at work or in pictures.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself doing anything over and over to try to make yourself look better or to deal with your concerns about your appearance?\n\nPatient: Yes. Before I teach a class or do one of those workshops at the senior center, I’ll stop in the bathroom and check the mirror a few times. I put lotion on my hands, turn my face different ways to see how it looks, and sometimes I’ll ask a coworker if I look okay. It can make me run a little late, but I still get myself there.\n\nDoctor: When you check the mirror and apply lotion, are you trying to make yourself look perfect or are you trying to stop yourself from looking a certain way that you think is unacceptable?\n\nPatient: It’s more like I’m trying not to look bad, if that makes sense. I don’t think I’m trying to look perfect. Before a class or one of the senior-center workshops, I’ll check the bathroom mirror a few times, put lotion on my hands, turn my face different ways, and sometimes ask a coworker if I look all right. It makes me run a little behind sometimes, but I still go.\n\nDoctor: Is there one particular part of your body that you're most concerned about?\n\nPatient: Mostly my hands. Since I started helping with the safety trainings, I notice how wrinkly and uneven they look when I’m pointing at things. And then sometimes it shifts to my face, like in the newsletter photos I think it looks crooked, even though people tell me they don’t see it.\n\nDoctor: Do these thoughts about your appearance come into your mind against your will?\n\nPatient: Yes, they do. I don’t really mean to think about it, but it just pops in my head. Since I started helping with those safety trainings, I keep noticing my hands when I’m pointing things out, like how wrinkly or uneven they look. And then it’ll move to my face in those newsletter photos, like I think it looks crooked, even when other people say they don’t see it.\n\nDoctor: Do these thoughts about your appearance interfere with your daily life or cause you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: It does bother me, yes. It hasn’t stopped me from going to work or volunteering, but I’m slower getting ready before trainings because I keep checking. And I’ve started avoiding photos when I can, or I hesitate before going to social things I used to just enjoy without thinking so much.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find it difficult to stop thinking about your appearance once these thoughts start?\n\nPatient: Yes. Once I notice it, it’s hard to let it go. Like with my hands during the safety trainings, I’ll keep thinking about how wrinkly or uneven they look when I’m pointing at things. Then it can switch to my face in those newsletter photos, thinking it looks crooked, even though people tell me they don’t see it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're able to control when these thoughts about your appearance come into your mind?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. It feels like it just comes in on its own. Since I started helping with the safety trainings, I’ll be pointing something out and suddenly I’m focused on how wrinkly or uneven my hands look. Then later I’ll see a newsletter photo and start thinking my face looks crooked, even if people tell me they don’t notice it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you have to think certain thoughts or repeat certain words to yourself to try to deal with your concerns about your appearance?\n\nPatient: Not really certain words, no. It’s more that I feel like I have to do certain things before a class or one of the senior-center workshops. I’ll check the bathroom mirror a few times, put lotion on my hands, turn my face different ways, and sometimes I’ll ask a coworker if I look all right. It makes me run a little behind sometimes, but I still get there.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of being ugly, or is it more that you're afraid of not looking your best?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more that I get stuck on looking wrong, not just “not my best.” Like, since I started helping with the safety trainings, I notice my hands when I’m pointing and they look wrinkly or uneven to me. And then I’ll see my face in those newsletter photos and think it looks crooked, even when other people say they don’t see it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. [
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n13\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF45.2, F42.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I came in because I’ve been getting really stuck on how I look. Since I started helping with the safety trainings at work, I keep noticing my hands when I’m pointing things out—how wrinkly and uneven they look. And then I’ll see my face in the company newsletter photos and think it looks crooked. Other people tell me they don’t see it, but I can’t seem to stop thinking about it.\n\nDoctor: Are you worried about anything else besides how you look?\n\nPatient: Not really anything else I can think of. It’s mostly been about the way I look, especially when I notice it at work or in pictures.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself doing anything over and over to try to make yourself look better or to deal with your concerns about your appearance?\n\nPatient: Yes. Before I teach a class or do one of those workshops at the senior center, I’ll stop in the bathroom and check the mirror a few times. I put lotion on my hands, turn my face different ways to see how it looks, and sometimes I’ll ask a coworker if I look okay. It can make me run a little late, but I still get myself there.\n\nDoctor: When you check the mirror and apply lotion, are you trying to make yourself look perfect or are you trying to stop yourself from looking a certain way that you think is unacceptable?\n\nPatient: It’s more like I’m trying not to look bad, if that makes sense. I don’t think I’m trying to look perfect. Before a class or one of the senior-center workshops, I’ll check the bathroom mirror a few times, put lotion on my hands, turn my face different ways, and sometimes ask a coworker if I look all right. It makes me run a little behind sometimes, but I still go.\n\nDoctor: Is there one particular part of your body that you're most concerned about?\n\nPatient: Mostly my hands. Since I started helping with the safety trainings, I notice how wrinkly and uneven they look when I’m pointing at things. And then sometimes it shifts to my face, like in the newsletter photos I think it looks crooked, even though people tell me they don’t see it.\n\nDoctor: Do these thoughts about your appearance come into your mind against your will?\n\nPatient: Yes, they do. I don’t really mean to think about it, but it just pops in my head. Since I started helping with those safety trainings, I keep noticing my hands when I’m pointing things out, like how wrinkly or uneven they look. And then it’ll move to my face in those newsletter photos, like I think it looks crooked, even when other people say they don’t see it.\n\nDoctor: Do these thoughts about your appearance interfere with your daily life or cause you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: It does bother me, yes. It hasn’t stopped me from going to work or volunteering, but I’m slower getting ready before trainings because I keep checking. And I’ve started avoiding photos when I can, or I hesitate before going to social things I used to just enjoy without thinking so much.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find it difficult to stop thinking about your appearance once these thoughts start?\n\nPatient: Yes. Once I notice it, it’s hard to let it go. Like with my hands during the safety trainings, I’ll keep thinking about how wrinkly or uneven they look when I’m pointing at things. Then it can switch to my face in those newsletter photos, thinking it looks crooked, even though people tell me they don’t see it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're able to control when these thoughts about your appearance come into your mind?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. It feels like it just comes in on its own. Since I started helping with the safety trainings, I’ll be pointing something out and suddenly I’m focused on how wrinkly or uneven my hands look. Then later I’ll see a newsletter photo and start thinking my face looks crooked, even if people tell me they don’t notice it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you have to think certain thoughts or repeat certain words to yourself to try to deal with your concerns about your appearance?\n\nPatient: Not really certain words, no. It’s more that I feel like I have to do certain things before a class or one of the senior-center workshops. I’ll check the bathroom mirror a few times, put lotion on my hands, turn my face different ways, and sometimes I’ll ask a coworker if I look all right. It makes me run a little behind sometimes, but I still get there.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of being ugly, or is it more that you're afraid of not looking your best?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more that I get stuck on looking wrong, not just “not my best.” Like, since I started helping with the safety trainings, I notice my hands when I’m pointing and they look wrinkly or uneven to me. And then I’ll see my face in those newsletter photos and think it looks crooked, even when other people say they don’t see it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your concerns about your appearance are reasonable, or do they seem irrational to you?\n\nPatient: Part of me knows it probably isn’t reasonable, because other people keep saying they don’t see what I’m seeing. But it still feels real to me when I notice my hands looking wrinkly or uneven, or when I see my face in those newsletter photos and it looks crooked to me. I know I get too stuck on it, I just have a hard time letting it go.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about your appearance are something you want to think about, or do they invade your mind without you wanting them to?\n\nPatient: They invade my mind, I’d say. I don’t want to sit there thinking about it, but since I started helping with the safety trainings, I’ll be pointing something out and suddenly I’m noticing how wrinkly or uneven my hands look. Then with the newsletter photos, I start thinking my face looks crooked, even though people tell me they don’t see it. It just kind of sticks in my head.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n14\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF45.2, F42.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I came in because I’ve been getting really stuck on how I look. Since I started helping with the safety trainings at work, I keep noticing my hands when I’m pointing things out—how wrinkly and uneven they look. And then I’ll see my face in the company newsletter photos and think it looks crooked. Other people tell me they don’t see it, but I can’t seem to stop thinking about it.\n\nDoctor: Are you worried about anything else besides how you look?\n\nPatient: Not really anything else I can think of. It’s mostly been about the way I look, especially when I notice it at work or in pictures.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself doing anything over and over to try to make yourself look better or to deal with your concerns about your appearance?\n\nPatient: Yes. Before I teach a class or do one of those workshops at the senior center, I’ll stop in the bathroom and check the mirror a few times. I put lotion on my hands, turn my face different ways to see how it looks, and sometimes I’ll ask a coworker if I look okay. It can make me run a little late, but I still get myself there.\n\nDoctor: When you check the mirror and apply lotion, are you trying to make yourself look perfect or are you trying to stop yourself from looking a certain way that you think is unacceptable?\n\nPatient: It’s more like I’m trying not to look bad, if that makes sense. I don’t think I’m trying to look perfect. Before a class or one of the senior-center workshops, I’ll check the bathroom mirror a few times, put lotion on my hands, turn my face different ways, and sometimes ask a coworker if I look all right. It makes me run a little behind sometimes, but I still go.\n\nDoctor: Is there one particular part of your body that you're most concerned about?\n\nPatient: Mostly my hands. Since I started helping with the safety trainings, I notice how wrinkly and uneven they look when I’m pointing at things. And then sometimes it shifts to my face, like in the newsletter photos I think it looks crooked, even though people tell me they don’t see it.\n\nDoctor: Do these thoughts about your appearance come into your mind against your will?\n\nPatient: Yes, they do. I don’t really mean to think about it, but it just pops in my head. Since I started helping with those safety trainings, I keep noticing my hands when I’m pointing things out, like how wrinkly or uneven they look. And then it’ll move to my face in those newsletter photos, like I think it looks crooked, even when other people say they don’t see it.\n\nDoctor: Do these thoughts about your appearance interfere with your daily life or cause you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: It does bother me, yes. It hasn’t stopped me from going to work or volunteering, but I’m slower getting ready before trainings because I keep checking. And I’ve started avoiding photos when I can, or I hesitate before going to social things I used to just enjoy without thinking so much.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find it difficult to stop thinking about your appearance once these thoughts start?\n\nPatient: Yes. Once I notice it, it’s hard to let it go. Like with my hands during the safety trainings, I’ll keep thinking about how wrinkly or uneven they look when I’m pointing at things. Then it can switch to my face in those newsletter photos, thinking it looks crooked, even though people tell me they don’t see it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're able to control when these thoughts about your appearance come into your mind?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. It feels like it just comes in on its own. Since I started helping with the safety trainings, I’ll be pointing something out and suddenly I’m focused on how wrinkly or uneven my hands look. Then later I’ll see a newsletter photo and start thinking my face looks crooked, even if people tell me they don’t notice it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you have to think certain thoughts or repeat certain words to yourself to try to deal with your concerns about your appearance?\n\nPatient: Not really certain words, no. It’s more that I feel like I have to do certain things before a class or one of the senior-center workshops. I’ll check the bathroom mirror a few times, put lotion on my hands, turn my face different ways, and sometimes I’ll ask a coworker if I look all right. It makes me run a little behind sometimes, but I still get there.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of being ugly, or is it more that you're afraid of not looking your best?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more that I get stuck on looking wrong, not just “not my best.” Like, since I started helping with the safety trainings, I notice my hands when I’m pointing and they look wrinkly or uneven to me. And then I’ll see my face in those newsletter photos and think it looks crooked, even when other people say they don’t see it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your concerns about your appearance are reasonable, or do they seem irrational to you?\n\nPatient: Part of me knows it probably isn’t reasonable, because other people keep saying they don’t see what I’m seeing. But it still feels real to me when I notice my hands looking wrinkly or uneven, or when I see my face in those newsletter photos and it looks crooked to me. I know I get too stuck on it, I just have a hard time letting it go.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about your appearance are something you want to think about, or do they invade your mind without you wanting them to?\n\nPatient: They invade my mind, I’d say. I don’t want to sit there thinking about it, but since I started helping with the safety trainings, I’ll be pointing something out and suddenly I’m noticing how wrinkly or uneven my hands look. Then with the newsletter photos, I start thinking my face looks crooked, even though people tell me they don’t see it. It just kind of sticks in my head.\n\nDoctor: Are you worried about what other people think of how you look, or is it more about how you feel about your own appearance?\n\nPatient: It’s more about how I feel about it, I think. I mean, I know other people say they don’t see anything wrong, but since I started helping with the safety trainings, I can’t stop noticing my hands when I’m pointing—how wrinkly or uneven they look to me. And then I see the newsletter photos and my face looks crooked to me. It just bothers me, even if other people aren’t thinking about it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Before a class or one of the senior-center workshops, I’ll check the bathroom mirror a few times, put lotion on my hands, turn my face different ways, and sometimes ask a coworker if I look all right. It makes me run a little behind sometimes, but I still go.\n\nDoctor: Is there one particular part of your body that you're most concerned about?\n\nPatient: Mostly my hands. Since I started helping with the safety trainings, I notice how wrinkly and uneven they look when I’m pointing at things. And then sometimes it shifts to my face, like in the newsletter photos I think it looks crooked, even though people tell me they don’t see it.\n\nDoctor: Do these thoughts about your appearance come into your mind against your will?\n\nPatient: Yes, they do. I don’t really mean to think about it, but it just pops in my head. Since I started helping with those safety trainings, I keep noticing my hands when I’m pointing things out, like how wrinkly or uneven they look. And then it’ll move to my face in those newsletter photos, like I think it looks crooked, even when other people say they don’t see it.\n\nDoctor: Do these thoughts about your appearance interfere with your daily life or cause you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: It does bother me, yes. It hasn’t stopped me from going to work or volunteering, but I’m slower getting ready before trainings because I keep checking. And I’ve started avoiding photos when I can, or I hesitate before going to social things I used to just enjoy without thinking so much.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find it difficult to stop thinking about your appearance once these thoughts start?\n\nPatient: Yes. Once I notice it, it’s hard to let it go. Like with my hands during the safety trainings, I’ll keep thinking about how wrinkly or uneven they look when I’m pointing at things. Then it can switch to my face in those newsletter photos, thinking it looks crooked, even though people tell me they don’t see it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're able to control when these thoughts about your appearance come into your mind?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. It feels like it just comes in on its own. Since I started helping with the safety trainings, I’ll be pointing something out and suddenly I’m focused on how wrinkly or uneven my hands look. Then later I’ll see a newsletter photo and start thinking my face looks crooked, even if people tell me they don’t notice it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you have to think certain thoughts or repeat certain words to yourself to try to deal with your concerns about your appearance?\n\nPatient: Not really certain words, no. It’s more that I feel like I have to do certain things before a class or one of the senior-center workshops. I’ll check the bathroom mirror a few times, put lotion on my hands, turn my face different ways, and sometimes I’ll ask a coworker if I look all right. It makes me run a little behind sometimes, but I still get there.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of being ugly, or is it more that you're afraid of not looking your best?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more that I get stuck on looking wrong, not just “not my best.” Like, since I started helping with the safety trainings, I notice my hands when I’m pointing and they look wrinkly or uneven to me. And then I’ll see my face in those newsletter photos and think it looks crooked, even when other people say they don’t see it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your concerns about your appearance are reasonable, or do they seem irrational to you?\n\nPatient: Part of me knows it probably isn’t reasonable, because other people keep saying they don’t see what I’m seeing. But it still feels real to me when I notice my hands looking wrinkly or uneven, or when I see my face in those newsletter photos and it looks crooked to me. I know I get too stuck on it, I just have a hard time letting it go.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about your appearance are something you want to think about, or do they invade your mind without you wanting them to?\n\nPatient: They invade my mind, I’d say. I don’t want to sit there thinking about it, but since I started helping with the safety trainings, I’ll be pointing something out and suddenly I’m noticing how wrinkly or uneven my hands look. Then with the newsletter photos, I start thinking my face looks crooked, even though people tell me they don’t see it. It just kind of sticks in my head.\n\nDoctor: Are you worried about what other people think of how you look, or is it more about how you feel about your own appearance?\n\nPatient: It’s more about how I feel about it, I think. I mean, I know other people say they don’t see anything wrong, but since I started helping with the safety trainings, I can’t stop noticing my hands when I’m pointing—how wrinkly or uneven they look to me. And then I see the newsletter photos and my face looks crooked to me. It just bothers me, even if other people aren’t thinking about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think that if you could fix the parts of your appearance that bother you, such as your hands or face, you would be able to stop worrying about how you look?\n\nPatient: I think I’d hope so, but I’m not sure it would really work that way. Since I started helping with the safety trainings, I just keep noticing my hands when I’m pointing things out—how wrinkly or uneven they look. And then if it wasn’t that, I worry I’d still look at the newsletter photos and think my face looks crooked, even though other people say they don’t see it. It feels like I’d want to fix it, but I don’t know if that would stop me from worrying.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

It can make me run a little late, but I still get myself there.\n\nDoctor: When you check the mirror and apply lotion, are you trying to make yourself look perfect or are you trying to stop yourself from looking a certain way that you think is unacceptable?\n\nPatient: It’s more like I’m trying not to look bad, if that makes sense. Since I started helping with the safety trainings, I notice how wrinkly and uneven they look when I’m pointing at things. I don’t really mean to think about it, but it just pops in my head. And then it’ll move to my face in those newsletter photos, like I think it looks crooked, even when other people say they don’t see it.\n\nDoctor: Do these thoughts about your appearance interfere with your daily life or cause you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: It does bother me, yes. It hasn’t stopped me from going to work or volunteering, but I’m slower getting ready before trainings because I keep checking. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I came in because I’ve been getting really stuck on how I look. Since I started helping with the safety trainings at work, I keep noticing my hands when I’m pointing things out—how wrinkly and uneven they look. And then I’ll see my face in the company newsletter photos and think it looks crooked. Other people tell me they don’t see it, but I can’t seem to stop thinking about it.\n\nDoctor: Are you worried about anything else besides how you look?\n\nPatient: Not really anything else I can think of. It’s mostly been about the way I look, especially when I notice it at work or in pictures.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself doing anything over and over to try to make yourself look better or to deal with your concerns about your appearance?\n\nPatient: Yes. Before I teach a class or do one of those workshops at the senior center, I’ll stop in the bathroom and check the mirror a few times. I put lotion on my hands, turn my face different ways to see how it looks, and sometimes I’ll ask a coworker if I look okay. It can make me run a little late, but I still get myself there.\n\nDoctor: When you check the mirror and apply lotion, are you trying to make yourself look perfect or are you trying to stop yourself from looking a certain way that you think is unacceptable?\n\nPatient: It’s more like I’m trying not to look bad, if that makes sense. I don’t think I’m trying to look perfect. Before a class or one of the senior-center workshops, I’ll check the bathroom mirror a few times, put lotion on my hands, turn my face different ways, and sometimes ask a coworker if I look all right. It makes me run a little behind sometimes, but I still go.\n\nDoctor: Is there one particular part of your body that you're most concerned about?\n\nPatient: Mostly my hands. Since I started helping with the safety trainings, I notice how wrinkly and uneven they look when I’m pointing at things. And then sometimes it shifts to my face, like in the newsletter photos I think it looks crooked, even though people tell me they don’t see it.\n\nDoctor: Do these thoughts about your appearance come into your mind against your will?\n\nPatient: Yes, they do. I don’t really mean to think about it, but it just pops in my head. Since I started helping with those safety trainings, I keep noticing my hands when I’m pointing things out, like how wrinkly or uneven they look. And then it’ll move to my face in those newsletter photos, like I think it looks crooked, even when other people say they don’t see it.\n\nDoctor: Do these thoughts about your appearance interfere with your daily life or cause you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: It does bother me, yes. It hasn’t stopped me from going to work or volunteering, but I’m slower getting ready before trainings because I keep checking. And I’ve started avoiding photos when I can, or I hesitate before going to social things I used to just enjoy without thinking so much.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find it difficult to stop thinking about your appearance once these thoughts start?\n\nPatient: Yes. Once I notice it, it’s hard to let it go. Like with my hands during the safety trainings, I’ll keep thinking about how wrinkly or uneven they look when I’m pointing at things. Then it can switch to my face in those newsletter photos, thinking it looks crooked, even though people tell me they don’t see it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're able to control when these thoughts about your appearance come into your mind?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. It feels like it just comes in on its own. Since I started helping with the safety trainings, I’ll be pointing something out and suddenly I’m focused on how wrinkly or uneven my hands look. Then later I’ll see a newsletter photo and start thinking my face looks crooked, even if people tell me they don’t notice it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you have to think certain thoughts or repeat certain words to yourself to try to deal with your concerns about your appearance?\n\nPatient: Not really certain words, no. It’s more that I feel like I have to do certain things before a class or one of the senior-center workshops. I’ll check the bathroom mirror a few times, put lotion on my hands, turn my face different ways, and sometimes I’ll ask a coworker if I look all right. It makes me run a little behind sometimes, but I still get there.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of being ugly, or is it more that you're afraid of not looking your best?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more that I get stuck on looking wrong, not just “not my best.” Like, since I started helping with the safety trainings, I notice my hands when I’m pointing and they look wrinkly or uneven to me. And then I’ll see my face in those newsletter photos and think it looks crooked, even when other people say they don’t see it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your concerns about your appearance are reasonable, or do they seem irrational to you?\n\nPatient: Part of me knows it probably isn’t reasonable, because other people keep saying they don’t see what I’m seeing. But it still feels real to me when I notice my hands looking wrinkly or uneven, or when I see my face in those newsletter photos and it looks crooked to me. I know I get too stuck on it, I just have a hard time letting it go.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about your appearance are something you want to think about, or do they invade your mind without you wanting them to?\n\nPatient: They invade my mind, I’d say. I don’t want to sit there thinking about it, but since I started helping with the safety trainings, I’ll be pointing something out and suddenly I’m noticing how wrinkly or uneven my hands look. Then with the newsletter photos, I start thinking my face looks crooked, even though people tell me they don’t see it. It just kind of sticks in my head.\n\nDoctor: Are you worried about what other people think of how you look, or is it more about how you feel about your own appearance?\n\nPatient: It’s more about how I feel about it, I think. I mean, I know other people say they don’t see anything wrong, but since I started helping with the safety trainings, I can’t stop noticing my hands when I’m pointing—how wrinkly or uneven they look to me. And then I see the newsletter photos and my face looks crooked to me. It just bothers me, even if other people aren’t thinking about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think that if you could fix the parts of your appearance that bother you, such as your hands or face, you would be able to stop worrying about how you look?\n\nPatient: I think I’d hope so, but I’m not sure it would really work that way. Since I started helping with the safety trainings, I just keep noticing my hands when I’m pointing things out—how wrinkly or uneven they look. And then if it wasn’t that, I worry I’d still look at the newsletter photos and think my face looks crooked, even though other people say they don’t see it. It feels like I’d want to fix it, but I don’t know if that would stop me from worrying.\n\nDoctor: Do you think that your appearance concerns are more about fixing a flaw that you believe is already there, or about preventing a flaw from developing?\n\nPatient: It feels more like I’m seeing flaws that I think are already there. Like with my hands, when I’m pointing during the safety trainings, I notice them looking wrinkly or uneven. And then in the newsletter photos, my face looks crooked to me, even though people tell me they don’t see it. It’s not so much that I’m worried something will develop later.\n\nDoctor: Are you worried that other people will judge you or think less of you because of how you look?\n\nPatient: A little, yes, but it’s mostly that I can’t stop noticing it myself. Since I started helping with the safety trainings, I keep seeing how wrinkly or uneven my hands look when I’m pointing things out. And then in the newsletter photos, my face looks crooked to me, even though other people say they don’t see it. So I do worry what people might think, but it starts with me seeing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think people would view you differently if they saw the flaws in your appearance that you notice?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think they might. I mean, when I’m doing the safety trainings and I’m pointing at things, I notice my hands so much—how wrinkly and uneven they look—and I start wondering if other people are seeing that too. And with the newsletter photos, my face looks crooked to me, even though people tell me they don’t notice it.
